I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I’ve really messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I can’t stop thinking I shouldn’t have done it, like I trapped everybody.

Yeah… probably. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.

Now I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.

And I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.

Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.

I came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.

Sometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.

Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.

Now I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.

And with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.

I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.

I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.

But yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.

And then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.

But for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.

It’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.

It’s not like one single day I can point to, but there are times it gets louder, especially when something small happens at work.\n\nLike if I mess up an order or drop something, I’ll be thinking about it way longer than I should, like, “See, this is what you do, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking about the mortgage again, how I’ve let my family down by taking that on. It all sort of connects in my head, even if it probably shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to do things, like put on a game or maybe sit outside for a bit, but it’s hard to tell if I’m looking forward to it or if I’m just doing it because I used to.\n\nLike the other day I thought about going to this diner I used to like after a shift, and then I started thinking about whether I should even spend money there, and then I remembered the owner changed the menu a while back, and it kind of turned into this whole thing in my head. So I’m not sure I’d say I really look forward to much. Maybe some things are okay in the moment, but I don’t really notice it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.\n\nNow I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, like you're useless and have messed things up, nearly every day for the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.\n\nSometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. And then other nights I get home and I’m just standing in the kitchen eating whatever’s there, not even like a real dinner, just trying to feel settled, I guess.\n\nSo… weight-wise, I’m not sure. But the eating has definitely been uneven.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about hurting yourself or wished you were dead during these past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.\n\nAnd then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely back to normal between these episodes of feeling useless and guilty?\n\nPatient: Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.\n\nBut for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

No, I don’t think so. Not completely.

I mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”

That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.

So… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment the way I should.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about messing things up and being useless?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I think about a year and a couple months, maybe around fourteen months? It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I thought it was just stress, you know, like everyone worries about bills and work.\n\nBut the feeling guilty, like I’ve messed everything up, and that useless feeling… that’s been around most of that time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last fourteen months where these feelings of guilt and uselessness were significantly worse than others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it kind of goes up and down, I guess. It’s not like one single day I can point to, but there are times it gets louder, especially when something small happens at work.\n\nLike if I mess up an order or drop something, I’ll be thinking about it way longer than I should, like, “See, this is what you do, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking about the mortgage again, how I’ve let my family down by taking that on. It all sort of connects in my head, even if it probably shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to do things, like put on a game or maybe sit outside for a bit, but it’s hard to tell if I’m looking forward to it or if I’m just doing it because I used to.\n\nLike the other day I thought about going to this diner I used to like after a shift, and then I started thinking about whether I should even spend money there, and then I remembered the owner changed the menu a while back, and it kind of turned into this whole thing in my head. So I’m not sure I’d say I really look forward to much. Maybe some things are okay in the moment, but I don’t really notice it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.\n\nNow I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, like you're useless and have messed things up, nearly every day for the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.\n\nSometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. And then other nights I get home and I’m just standing in the kitchen eating whatever’s there, not even like a real dinner, just trying to feel settled, I guess.\n\nSo… weight-wise, I’m not sure. But the eating has definitely been uneven.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about hurting yourself or wished you were dead during these past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.\n\nAnd then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely back to normal between these episodes of feeling useless and guilty?\n\nPatient: Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.\n\nBut for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F32.1", "F33.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute, because sometimes I’m taking an order or carrying plates and I’m thinking about, like, who needs coffee refilled.

But it comes back really easily. Like with the mortgage, I keep thinking I let my family down taking that on, like I should’ve known better. And then if I do something small at work—forget ketchup, mix up a table, something stupid—it feels like proof, you know? Like, “There it is, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking I kind of deserve whatever happens from it.

Sorry, I’m probably not answering straight. It’s not just once in a while, though. It hangs around.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I’ve really messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I can’t stop thinking I shouldn’t have done it, like I trapped everybody.\n\nAnd then at work, if I drop something or get an order wrong—I'm a waiter, so it happens, you know, people change their minds, the kitchen gets backed up—but when I do it, it feels like proof that I’m just useless. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… I don’t know. It all sort of connects in my head, even if it probably shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to do things, like put on a game or maybe sit outside for a bit, but it’s hard to tell if I’m looking forward to it or if I’m just doing it because I used to.\n\nLike the other day I thought about going to this diner I used to like after a shift, and then I started thinking about whether I should even spend money there, and then I remembered the owner changed the menu a while back, and it kind of turned into this whole thing in my head. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. Yeah… most days, I’d say.

It all sort of connects in my head, even if it probably shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to do things, like put on a game or maybe sit outside for a bit, but it’s hard to tell if I’m looking forward to it or if I’m just doing it because I used to.\n\nLike the other day I thought about going to this diner I used to like after a shift, and then I started thinking about whether I should even spend money there, and then I remembered the owner changed the menu a while back, and it kind of turned into this whole thing in my head. So I’m not sure I’d say I really look forward to much. Maybe some things are okay in the moment, but I don’t really notice it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.\n\nNow I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, like you're useless and have messed things up, nearly every day for the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.\n\nSometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. And then other nights I get home and I’m just standing in the kitchen eating whatever’s there, not even like a real dinner, just trying to feel settled, I guess.\n\nSo… weight-wise, I’m not sure. But the eating has definitely been uneven.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about hurting yourself or wished you were dead during these past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.\n\nAnd then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely back to normal between these episodes of feeling useless and guilty?\n\nPatient: Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.\n\nBut for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of guilt and uselessness something that you experience throughout most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute, because sometimes I’m taking an order or carrying plates and I’m thinking about, like, who needs coffee refilled.\n\nBut it comes back really easily. Like with the mortgage, I keep thinking I let my family down taking that on, like I should’ve known better. And then if I do something small at work—forget ketchup, mix up a table, something stupid—it feels like proof, you know? Like, “There it is, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking I kind of deserve whatever happens from it.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering straight. It’s not just once in a while, though. It hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been depressed most days for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, empty feeling, kind of sitting in my chest. Like I’ll be at work carrying plates around and smiling, doing the whole polite waiter thing, and people probably think I’m fine because I’m saying the right words. But it doesn’t feel like I’m actually fine. It feels more like I’m just moving because I’m supposed to.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll get distracted if a table needs something or the kitchen’s backed up—sorry, there was this one brunch shift where everyone wanted substitutions and it was chaos—but underneath it, yeah, most days these past couple weeks it’s been there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.

I mean, there were lighter days, sure. Like if work was busy, or I got home and just watched something dumb on TV, sometimes I could sort of not think about it for a while. Actually, there was this one day I remember because the dishwasher broke at the restaurant and everyone was scrambling, and I was just focused on that mess instead of myself.

I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.\n\nNow I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, like you're useless and have messed things up, nearly every day for the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.\n\nSometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. And then other nights I get home and I’m just standing in the kitchen eating whatever’s there, not even like a real dinner, just trying to feel settled, I guess.\n\nSo… weight-wise, I’m not sure. But the eating has definitely been uneven.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about hurting yourself or wished you were dead during these past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.\n\nAnd then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely back to normal between these episodes of feeling useless and guilty?\n\nPatient: Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.\n\nBut for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of guilt and uselessness something that you experience throughout most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute, because sometimes I’m taking an order or carrying plates and I’m thinking about, like, who needs coffee refilled.\n\nBut it comes back really easily. Like with the mortgage, I keep thinking I let my family down taking that on, like I should’ve known better. And then if I do something small at work—forget ketchup, mix up a table, something stupid—it feels like proof, you know? Like, “There it is, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking I kind of deserve whatever happens from it.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering straight. It’s not just once in a while, though. It hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been depressed most days for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, empty feeling, kind of sitting in my chest. Like I’ll be at work carrying plates around and smiling, doing the whole polite waiter thing, and people probably think I’m fine because I’m saying the right words. But it doesn’t feel like I’m actually fine. It feels more like I’m just moving because I’m supposed to.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll get distracted if a table needs something or the kitchen’s backed up—sorry, there was this one brunch shift where everyone wanted substitutions and it was chaos—but underneath it, yeah, most days these past couple weeks it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how brief, in the last fourteen months where you felt completely free of these depressive feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.\n\nI mean, there were lighter days, sure. Like if work was busy, or I got home and just watched something dumb on TV, sometimes I could sort of not think about it for a while. Actually, there was this one day I remember because the dishwasher broke at the restaurant and everyone was scrambling, and I was just focused on that mess instead of myself.\n\nBut even then, it’s like it comes back underneath everything. For most of this whole stretch, I’ve felt weighed down by that empty, guilty feeling more often than not. Not every second, but… it hasn’t really let me be totally free of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It feels more like I’m just moving because I’m supposed to.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll get distracted if a table needs something or the kitchen’s backed up—sorry, there was this one brunch shift where everyone wanted substitutions and it was chaos—but underneath it, yeah, most days these past couple weeks it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how brief, in the last fourteen months where you felt completely free of these depressive feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.\n\nI mean, there were lighter days, sure. Like if work was busy, or I got home and just watched something dumb on TV, sometimes I could sort of not think about it for a while. Actually, there was this one day I remember because the dishwasher broke at the restaurant and everyone was scrambling, and I was just focused on that mess instead of myself.\n\nBut even then, it’s like it comes back underneath everything. For most of this whole stretch, I’ve felt weighed down by that empty, guilty feeling more often than not. Not every second, but… it hasn’t really let me be totally free of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had at least two weeks in the past fourteen months where you didn't feel depressed at all?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

There was… I mean, maybe there was a stretch of a few weeks where I wasn’t especially depressed. Like I was getting through work better, not dragging myself quite as much.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I’ve really messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I can’t stop thinking I shouldn’t have done it, like I trapped everybody.\n\nAnd then at work, if I drop something or get an order wrong—I'm a waiter, so it happens, you know, people change their minds, the kitchen gets backed up—but when I do it, it feels like proof that I’m just useless. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. Sorry, I know that’s probably not a straight answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like, I used to enjoy little things after work, but lately when I try to think about “pleasure,” I kind of get stuck on whether I’m even doing things the right way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll put something on TV, but then I start thinking about the house payment or whether I forgot something at work, and then I’m not really watching it anymore. So… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment the way I should.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about messing things up and being useless?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I think about a year and a couple months, maybe around fourteen months? It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I thought it was just stress, you know, like everyone worries about bills and work.\n\nBut the feeling guilty, like I’ve messed everything up, and that useless feeling… that’s been around most of that time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last fourteen months where these feelings of guilt and uselessness were significantly worse than others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it kind of goes up and down, I guess. It’s not like one single day I can point to, but there are times it gets louder, especially when something small happens at work.\n\nLike if I mess up an order or drop something, I’ll be thinking about it way longer than I should, like, “See, this is what you do, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking about the mortgage again, how I’ve let my family down by taking that on. It all sort of connects in my head, even if it probably shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to do things, like put on a game or maybe sit outside for a bit, but it’s hard to tell if I’m looking forward to it or if I’m just doing it because I used to.\n\nLike the other day I thought about going to this diner I used to like after a shift, and then I started thinking about whether I should even spend money there, and then I remembered the owner changed the menu a while back, and it kind of turned into this whole thing in my head. So I’m not sure I’d say I really look forward to much. Maybe some things are okay in the moment, but I don’t really notice it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.\n\nNow I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, like you're useless and have messed things up, nearly every day for the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.\n\nSometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. And then other nights I get home and I’m just standing in the kitchen eating whatever’s there, not even like a real dinner, just trying to feel settled, I guess.\n\nSo… weight-wise, I’m not sure. But the eating has definitely been uneven.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about hurting yourself or wished you were dead during these past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.\n\nAnd then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely back to normal between these episodes of feeling useless and guilty?\n\nPatient: Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.\n\nBut for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of guilt and uselessness something that you experience throughout most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of guilt and uselessness something that you experience throughout most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute, because sometimes I’m taking an order or carrying plates and I’m thinking about, like, who needs coffee refilled.\n\nBut it comes back really easily. Like with the mortgage, I keep thinking I let my family down taking that on, like I should’ve known better. And then if I do something small at work—forget ketchup, mix up a table, something stupid—it feels like proof, you know? Like, “There it is, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking I kind of deserve whatever happens from it.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering straight. It’s not just once in a while, though. It hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been depressed most days for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, empty feeling, kind of sitting in my chest. It feels more like I’m just moving because I’m supposed to.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll get distracted if a table needs something or the kitchen’s backed up—sorry, there was this one brunch shift where everyone wanted substitutions and it was chaos—but underneath it, yeah, most days these past couple weeks it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how brief, in the last fourteen months where you felt completely free of these depressive feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.\n\nI mean, there were lighter days, sure. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of guilt and uselessness something that you experience throughout most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute, because sometimes I’m taking an order or carrying plates and I’m thinking about, like, who needs coffee refilled.\n\nBut it comes back really easily. Like with the mortgage, I keep thinking I let my family down taking that on, like I should’ve known better. And then if I do something small at work—forget ketchup, mix up a table, something stupid—it feels like proof, you know? Like, “There it is, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking I kind of deserve whatever happens from it.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering straight. It’s not just once in a while, though. It hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been depressed most days for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, empty feeling, kind of sitting in my chest. It feels more like I’m just moving because I’m supposed to.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll get distracted if a table needs something or the kitchen’s backed up—sorry, there was this one brunch shift where everyone wanted substitutions and it was chaos—but underneath it, yeah, most days these past couple weeks it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how brief, in the last fourteen months where you felt completely free of these depressive feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.\n\nI mean, there were lighter days, sure. Like if work was busy, or I got home and just watched something dumb on TV, sometimes I could sort of not think about it for a while. Actually, there was this one day I remember because the dishwasher broke at the restaurant and everyone was scrambling, and I was just focused on that mess instead of myself.\n\nBut even then, it’s like it comes back underneath everything. For most of this whole stretch, I’ve felt weighed down by that empty, guilty feeling more often than not. Not every second, but… it hasn’t really let me be totally free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks in the past fourteen months where you didn't feel depressed at all?\n\nPatient: There was… I mean, maybe there was a stretch of a few weeks where I wasn’t especially depressed. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how brief, in the last fourteen months where you felt completely free of these depressive feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.\n\nI mean, there were lighter days, sure. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really like that, no. I mean, I keep trying to think of it like chapters, like depressed, then normal, then depressed again, but it hasn’t felt that neat.

There are lighter days, sure. Sometimes I’ll get distracted by work or errands or, you know, something stupid like trying to fix a leaky faucet and suddenly three hours are gone. But for most of this whole period, even when some days were lighter, I’ve still felt weighed down by that empty, guilty feeling more often than not. It’s kind of just been there underneath things.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I keep feeling like I’ve really messed things up for my family. We took on this mortgage, and I can’t stop thinking I shouldn’t have done it, like I trapped everybody.\n\nAnd then at work, if I drop something or get an order wrong—I'm a waiter, so it happens, you know, people change their minds, the kitchen gets backed up—but when I do it, it feels like proof that I’m just useless. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. Sorry, I know that’s probably not a straight answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like, I used to enjoy little things after work, but lately when I try to think about “pleasure,” I kind of get stuck on whether I’m even doing things the right way, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll put something on TV, but then I start thinking about the house payment or whether I forgot something at work, and then I’m not really watching it anymore. So… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t really been paying attention to enjoyment the way I should.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about messing things up and being useless?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I think about a year and a couple months, maybe around fourteen months? It’s hard to pin down exactly because at first I thought it was just stress, you know, like everyone worries about bills and work.\n\nBut the feeling guilty, like I’ve messed everything up, and that useless feeling… that’s been around most of that time.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the last fourteen months where these feelings of guilt and uselessness were significantly worse than others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it kind of goes up and down, I guess. It’s not like one single day I can point to, but there are times it gets louder, especially when something small happens at work.\n\nLike if I mess up an order or drop something, I’ll be thinking about it way longer than I should, like, “See, this is what you do, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking about the mortgage again, how I’ve let my family down by taking that on. It all sort of connects in my head, even if it probably shouldn’t.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still look forward to or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I try to do things, like put on a game or maybe sit outside for a bit, but it’s hard to tell if I’m looking forward to it or if I’m just doing it because I used to.\n\nLike the other day I thought about going to this diner I used to like after a shift, and then I started thinking about whether I should even spend money there, and then I remembered the owner changed the menu a while back, and it kind of turned into this whole thing in my head. So I’m not sure I’d say I really look forward to much. Maybe some things are okay in the moment, but I don’t really notice it clearly.\n\nDoctor: Are there things you used to enjoy that you no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yeah… probably. I used to kind of enjoy talking with the regulars at work, joking around with them, remembering what they ordered, that sort of thing. It made the shift go by faster.\n\nNow I mostly just… keep my face straight and say what I need to say. Like, “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” that’s it. I don’t really feel that little spark to joke around.\n\nAnd I used to text my brother after shifts sometimes, just dumb stuff, like something funny that happened at the restaurant or whatever. Now his messages sit there. I see them, and I think I should answer, but I just don’t feel pulled to do it. Then later it’s been too long and it feels weird, so I still don’t.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, like you're useless and have messed things up, nearly every day for the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… nearly every day, I think. It’s kind of there in the background all the time.\n\nI came in because I keep feeling like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage, like I made this big decision and now everyone’s stuck with it because of me. And then at work, if I mess up something small—like an order or I forget a table needs coffee—it feels like proof, you know? Like, there it is again, I’m useless.\n\nSometimes I know it sounds bigger than the mistake, because it’s just a restaurant thing, people forget stuff, but in my head it turns into… like I deserve whatever happens after that. I don’t really shake it off the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Like with the regulars at work, I used to actually care about that little back-and-forth, joking with them, remembering their usual order. It sounds small, but it made me feel like I was part of the place.\n\nNow I mostly just keep my face straight and say what I have to say. “What can I get you,” “I’ll be right back,” stuff like that. I don’t really get into it.\n\nAnd with my brother, too—I used to text him after shifts, just dumb things that happened, like if somebody made a weird complaint or left a funny note. Now I see his messages and just… let them sit there. Not because I’m mad at him. I just don’t feel that pull to answer, and then the time passes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s been a significant amount, like I haven’t really been tracking it or anything. My eating’s just been… all over the place.\n\nSome weeks at work I barely touch the staff meal, even if it’s something I’d normally eat. I’ll just pick at it and then go back out on the floor. And then other nights I get home and I’m just standing in the kitchen eating whatever’s there, not even like a real dinner, just trying to feel settled, I guess.\n\nSo… weight-wise, I’m not sure. But the eating has definitely been uneven.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about hurting yourself or wished you were dead during these past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: I mean… I haven’t made a plan or anything. I don’t sit there thinking, like, “I’m going to do this.” It’s not like that.\n\nBut yeah, sometimes when I’m lying there at night—especially if I’ve got an early shift and I know I’m going to be tired and probably mess something up again—I’ll think… it would just be easier if I didn’t wake up. Like if I could just not have to keep doing this, not keep failing everybody.\n\nAnd then the alarm goes off anyway, and I get up, because tables aren’t going to wait, you know? People want coffee at 6:30 like it’s life or death. Sorry, that’s not really the point. But… yeah, those thoughts come up sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt completely back to normal between these episodes of feeling useless and guilty?\n\nPatient: Not really completely back to normal, no. I mean, there are days where it’s not as heavy, like I can get through a shift and not be stuck in my head the whole time, or I’ll laugh at something someone says.\n\nBut for most of this whole stretch, even when it lightens up a little, that empty feeling is still kind of sitting there. And the guilt too, like I’m not doing enough or I’m letting people down. It’s more often there than not.\n\nIt’s funny, people at work probably wouldn’t know, because you can refill coffee and smile at somebody and still feel… I don’t know, hollow. So no, I wouldn’t say I’ve had real periods where I felt like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you no longer enjoy things that you used to find pleasurable, most of the time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Like… I still like some things, I think. If someone puts on a game at the bar after my shift, I’ll watch for a bit, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left the ketchup bottles half-filled or if somebody’s going to say something the next day.\n\nI used to be better at just enjoying stuff without making it into a whole thing. But “most of the time”… I don’t know. I haven’t really been keeping track like that. Sometimes things feel kind of flat, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt consistently depressed for most of the past fourteen months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Most days it’s just this heavy, empty feeling, like right in my chest.\n\nI’ll be carrying plates around and smiling because that’s what you do, you know, people don’t want their eggs with some miserable guy hovering over them. And sometimes I catch myself doing the whole waiter voice, like “Can I get you anything else?” and it feels like I’m just… going through the motions. Not because I feel okay or anything good, just because I’m supposed to keep moving. So, yeah, most of the time it’s been like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods, no matter how short, where you felt completely back to your normal self, without any of these feelings of guilt or uselessness?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not completely.\n\nI mean, I can have a decent few hours, maybe if work is busy and I’m just focused on tables, or if somebody jokes around with me. But then something small happens, like I forget an order of toast or mix up who wanted decaf, and it’s right back to, “See, you’re useless.”\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. I keep feeling like I let my family down with the mortgage, like I took on something I had no business taking on. And then every little mistake at work feels like proof of it. Like I deserve whatever comes from it. So… no, I don’t really get a clean break from that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of guilt and uselessness something that you experience throughout most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s… it’s around a lot of the day, yeah. I don’t know if I’d say every single minute, because sometimes I’m taking an order or carrying plates and I’m thinking about, like, who needs coffee refilled.\n\nBut it comes back really easily. Like with the mortgage, I keep thinking I let my family down taking that on, like I should’ve known better. And then if I do something small at work—forget ketchup, mix up a table, something stupid—it feels like proof, you know? Like, “There it is, you’re useless.” And then I start thinking I kind of deserve whatever happens from it.\n\nSorry, I’m probably not answering straight. It’s not just once in a while, though. It hangs around.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been depressed most days for the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say.\n\nIt’s that same heavy, empty feeling, kind of sitting in my chest. Like I’ll be at work carrying plates around and smiling, doing the whole polite waiter thing, and people probably think I’m fine because I’m saying the right words. But it doesn’t feel like I’m actually fine. It feels more like I’m just moving because I’m supposed to.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll get distracted if a table needs something or the kitchen’s backed up—sorry, there was this one brunch shift where everyone wanted substitutions and it was chaos—but underneath it, yeah, most days these past couple weeks it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any period, no matter how brief, in the last fourteen months where you felt completely free of these depressive feelings?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there’s been a time where it was completely gone.\n\nI mean, there were lighter days, sure. Like if work was busy, or I got home and just watched something dumb on TV, sometimes I could sort of not think about it for a while. Actually, there was this one day I remember because the dishwasher broke at the restaurant and everyone was scrambling, and I was just focused on that mess instead of myself.\n\nBut even then, it’s like it comes back underneath everything. For most of this whole stretch, I’ve felt weighed down by that empty, guilty feeling more often than not. Not every second, but… it hasn’t really let me be totally free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had at least two weeks in the past fourteen months where you didn't feel depressed at all?\n\nPatient: There was… I mean, maybe there was a stretch of a few weeks where I wasn’t especially depressed.
